Global-in-time Discontinuous Solutions for the Two-Phase Model of Compressible Fluids with Density-Dependent Viscosity
Marcel Zodji

Abstract
. We are concerned with a model describing the motion of two compressible, immiscible fluids with density-dependent viscosity in the whole ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . The phases of the flow may have different pressure and viscosity laws and are separated by a sharp interface, across which the (total) density is discontinuous. Our goal is to study the persistence of the regularity of this sharp interface over time. More precisely, the dynamics of the flow are governed by three coupled equations: two hyperbolic equations (for the volume fraction of one phase and for the density) and a parabolic equation for the velocity field. We assume that, at the initial time, the density is α \alpha -Hölder continuous on both sides of a 𝒞 1 + α \mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha} -regular surface across which it may be discontinuous. We prove the existence and uniqueness of a global-in-time weak solution in an intermediate regularity class that ensures the persistence of the piecewise Hölder regularity of the density and the 𝒞 1 + α \mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha} regularity of the sharp interface.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
76T10, 76N10, 35Q30, 35Q35

1.
Introduction

This paper deals with the following system of PDEs describing the motion of two compressible viscous fluids with density-dependent viscosity in the three-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c+u\cdot\nabla c=0,\\
\partial_{t}\rho+\dvg(\rho u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\dvg(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla P(\rho,c)=\dvg\big(2\mu(\rho,c)\mathbb{D}u\big)+\nabla\big(\lambda(\rho,c)\dvg u\big).\end{cases}

Above,
c=c(t,x)\in[0,1]
denotes the volume fraction,
\rho=\rho(t,x)\in[0,\infty)
is the total density, and
u=u(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
represents the single velocity field of the fluid mixture. These are the unknowns of the problem. Meanwhile,
P=P(\rho,c)
,
\mu=\mu(\rho,c)
, and
\lambda=\lambda(\rho,c)
are respectively the pressure, shear viscosity, and bulk viscosity laws of the mixture. They are given
W_{\text{loc}}^{2,\infty}
-regular functions of
\rho
and
c
. The derivation of system (
1.1
) from the classical two-fluid model can be found in
[
44
]
. Specifically, this system corresponds to the single-velocity Baer-Nunziato model, where
\rho_{+}=\rho c
and
\rho_{-}=\rho(1-c)
represent the densities of the fluid phases.

For initially immiscible flows, each phase occupies its own domain, and they are separated by a sharp interface in such a way that we have
{\rho_{+}}_{|t=0}{\rho_{-}}_{|t=0}=0
, or equivalently (for positive total density)
c_{0}(1-c_{0})=0
. In order words, the volume fraction
c_{0}
is the characteristic function of a domain
D_{0}
. Based on DiPerna–Lions theory (see e.g
[
13
,
40
]
), any weak solution of (
1.1
) in the classical energy framework, with
\dvg u\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, preserves the patch structure of the volume fraction (and hence the immiscibility of the phases):

(1.2)

c(1-c)=0,

so that
c(t)
remains the characteristic function of a domain
D(t)
. In general,
D(t)
corresponds to the image
of the initial domain
D_{0}
under the flow map associated with the fluid velocity.

In the case where both phases have the same constant viscosity coefficients, the existence of weak solutions in the classical energy framework has been the subject of several works since 2019 (see, for instance,
[
40
, Vasseur, Wen and Yu]
,
[
31
, Krac̆mar, Kwon, Nec̆esová and Novotný]
,
[
37
, Novotný]
,
[
38
, Novotný and Pokorný]
,
[
42
, Wen]
, and others). The velocity field is relatively weak
in the sense that it is only known to satisfy

(1.3)

\sqrt{\rho}u\in L^{\infty}((0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\nabla u\in L^{2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}).

This regularity is not sufficient to define a flow map for the velocity field, and thus to describe the dynamics of the sharp interface that separates the fluid phases. Conversely, classical solutions (see, for instance,
[
10
,
41
]
) are too strong, in the sense that they do not allow for discontinuities in the densities or in the volume fraction.

The main purpose of this paper is to construct global-in-time weak solutions of (
1.1
) in an intermediate regularity class which allow for the immiscibility of the fluid phases and for the persistence of the regularity of the sharp interface over time.
In Section
1.1
below, we state our main results, which are then compared to existing results in Section
1.2
.

1.1.
Main Results

This section is devoted to the statement of the main results. To simplify the presentation, we first specify the assumptions on the initial data and on the pressure and viscosity laws.

Assumptions on the pressure and viscosity laws

Given that the density of the phase labeled
"+"
(resp.
"-"
) is
\rho_{+}=\rho c
(resp.
\rho_{-}=\rho(1-c)
), if
P_{\pm}
denotes the pressure law of the phase
"\pm"
, then the pressure
P=P(\rho,c)
in (
1.1
) is defined as follows:

(1.4)

\displaystyle P(\rho,c)=P_{+}(\rho c)+P_{-}(\rho(1-c)).

A similar definition holds for the shear and bulk viscosity laws.

We always assume that the shear viscosity is strictly positive, the bulk viscosity is non-negative, and the pressure is an increasing function of the density: for all
\rho>0
,
c\in\{0,1\}

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mu(\rho,c)>0,\quad\lambda(\rho,c)\geqslant 0,\quad\partial_{\rho}P(\rho,c)>0,

Following our computations, we believe that the non-negativity assumption on the bulk viscosity can be waived.

We identify two particular cases where the algebraic structure of the model allows for proving that the pressure and shear viscosity jumps are stable: either

\diamond

the shear viscosity depends only on the volume fraction,
\mu(\rho,c)=\mu(c)
and there exists a
W^{2,\infty}_{\text{loc}}
-regular function
\Psi_{P}
such that

(1.6)

\displaystyle P(\rho,c)=\Psi_{P}\big(f(\rho,c)\big),

where the
(\rho,c)
-dependent function
f
satisfies

(1.7)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{\rho}f(\rho,c)=\nu(\rho,c),\;\text{ and where }\;\nu(\rho,c)=2\mu(\rho,c)+\lambda(\rho,c)

denotes the total viscosity. Differentiating (
1.6
) with respect to
\rho
, we obtain

(1.8)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}_{P}\big(f(\rho,c)\big)=\frac{\rho\partial_{\rho}P(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}.

\diamond

or the shear viscosity of one phase depends on the density (namely
\partial_{\rho}\mu(\rho,c)\not\equiv 0
) and there exist
W^{2,\infty}_{\text{loc}}
-regular functions
\Psi_{P}
, and
\Psi_{\mu}
such that

(1.9)

\displaystyle P(\rho,c)=\Psi_{P}\big(f(\rho,c)\big),\text{ and }\mu(\rho,c)=\Psi_{\mu}\big(f(\rho,c)\big).

In fact, in the latter situation, the jump of the pressure and shear viscosity at the sharp interface decay
exponentially in time. If, in addition to (
1.9
), there exists a
W^{2,\infty}_{\text{loc}}
-regular function
\Psi_{\lambda}
such that

(1.10)

\displaystyle\lambda(\rho,c)=\Psi_{\lambda}\big(f(\rho,c)\big),

then the jump of the viscosity bulk viscosity and velocity gradient decay exponentially in time as well.
Assumptions (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) hold true, for example, when
\mu
,
\lambda
, and
P
are independent of the volume fraction, which corresponds to the Navier–Stokes model. Moreover, for power constitutive laws

P_{\pm}(\rho)=b_{\pm}\rho^{\gamma_{\pm}},\quad\nu_{\pm}(\rho)=a_{\pm}\rho^{\alpha_{\pm}},\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma_{\pm}\in[1,\infty),\;\text{ and }\;b_{\pm},a_{\pm},\alpha_{\pm}\in(0,\infty),

the assumption (
1.6
) holds if

\frac{\gamma_{+}}{\alpha_{+}}=\frac{\gamma_{-}}{\alpha_{-}}=\check{\gamma},\;\text{ and }\;b_{+}\Bigg(\frac{\alpha_{+}}{a_{+}}\Bigg)^{\check{\gamma}}=b_{-}\Bigg(\frac{\alpha_{-}}{a_{-}}\Bigg)^{\check{\gamma}}=\check{b},\;\text{ and we have }\;\Psi_{P}(\varrho)=\check{b}\varrho^{\check{\gamma}}.

Assumptions on the initial data

We consider the system (
1.1
), supplemented with initial conditions

(1.11)

\displaystyle(c,\rho,\rho u)_{|t=0}=(c_{0},\rho_{0},\rho_{0}u_{0})

satisfying the following assumptions: there exists

\diamond

a simply connected, bounded and open domain
D_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of class
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
, such that

(1.12)

c_{0}=\mathbb{1}_{D_{0}^{c}}.

\diamond

a constant pressure equilibrium
\widetilde{P}>0
such that

(1.13)

P(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{P}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\,\text{ and }\,u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\;\text{ }\rho_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\;\text{ }\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)>0.

\diamond

some
p_{0}\in(1,6/5)
, such that

(1.14)

P(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{P},\rho_{0}u_{0}\in L^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

This (low-frequency) assumption allows us to derive time-decay estimates for the solutions.
Finally, we consider a shear-viscosity equilibrium state,
\widetilde{\mu}
, such that:

\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{\mu}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

What we mean by the
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
regularity of
D_{0}
is the following.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
D
be an open, bounded and simply connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with boundary
\Sigma=\partial D
.

We say that
D
(or
\Sigma
) is of class
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
if there exist a finite number of open sets
B_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
j=1,\dots J
, and maps
\tau_{j}\colon B_{j}\longrightarrow\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

\diamond

\tau_{j}
are
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular, and are homeomorphisms onto their images; and
\Sigma=\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}\tau_{j}(B_{j})
.

\diamond

for all
j,k
such that
\tau_{j}(B_{j})\cap\tau_{k}(B_{k})\neq\emptyset
, the map
\tau_{j}^{-1}\circ\tau_{k}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
-diffeomorphism from

\tau_{k}^{-1}\big(\tau_{j}(B_{j})\cap\tau_{k}(B_{k})\big)
onto
\tau_{j}^{-1}\big(\tau_{j}(B_{j})\cap\tau_{k}(B_{k})\big)
.

We introduce the following notations to quantify the regularity of
\Sigma
(or
\tau_{j}
if preferred):

(1.15)

\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}=\sup_{j}\left\lVert\nabla\tau_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}},\;\big|\Sigma\big|_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}=\sup_{j}\left\lvert\nabla\tau_{j}\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}},\;\text{ and }\;\;\big|\Sigma\big|_{\text{inf}}=\inf_{j}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\neq s^{\prime}\\
s,s\in B_{j}\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\tau_{j}(s)-\tau_{j}(s^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert s-s^{\prime}\right\rvert}\Bigg)\in(0,\infty).

Furthermore, there exists
1
1

1

Following
[
30
]
, we construct
\varphi
as solution to
-\Delta\varphi=g
in
D
, and
\varphi=0
on
\Sigma
, where
0<g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D)
. Standard estimates for elliptic equations (see, for instance,
[
21
]
) imply that
0<\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}(D)
. The fact that
\left\lvert\nabla\varphi\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}>0
is a consequence of Hopf’s lemma at boundary points, which holds for
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular manifolds (see
[
15
]
).
a
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\varphi=0\text{ on }\Sigma,\;\;D=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\colon\varphi(x)>0\big\},\;\text{ and }\;\left\lvert\nabla\varphi\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}=\inf_{x\in\Sigma}\left\lvert\nabla\varphi(x)\right\rvert>0.

Since
\varphi\circ\tau_{j}=0
on
B_{j}
, the outward unit normal vector field to
D
is given by

(1.16)

\displaystyle n_{x}(\tau_{j}(s))=\frac{\nabla\varphi(\tau_{j}(s))}{\left\lvert\nabla\varphi(\tau_{j}(s))\right\rvert}.

Recall that the relevant situations for the immiscible two-phase model is when the total density
\rho=\rho_{+}+\rho_{-}
is discontinuous. Since the densities
\rho_{\pm}
of the fluid phases are different and
\rho_{+}\rho_{-}=0
, the class of piecewise regular functions (for the total density
\rho
) is suitable for the study of (
1.1
).

In this paper, we will work with the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of discontinuous functions on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous on either side of
\Sigma
(recall that
\mathbb{R}^{3}=D\cup\Sigma\cup(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{D})
):

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\Big\{g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\colon g\in\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\Big\},\;\text{ where }\;\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{D})\cap\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus D).

The semi-norm (resp. norm) associated with
\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
(resp.
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
) is defined as follows:

\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{D})}+\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus D)}\quad\big(\text{resp.}\quad\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\left\lvert g\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\big).

Given a function
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we define its traces
g^{+}
and
g^{-}
on
\Sigma
, along with its average
\left\langle g\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}
and its jump
\llbracket g\rrbracket
across
\Sigma
, as follows: for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
,

(1.17)

\displaystyle g^{+}(\sigma)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}g\big(\sigma+rn_{x}(\sigma)\big),\;\,g^{-}(\sigma)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}g\big(\sigma-rn_{x}(\sigma)\big),\;\,\left\langle g\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}=\frac{g^{+}+g^{-}}{2},\quad\llbracket g\rrbracket=g^{+}-g^{-}.

In addition to the assumptions (
1.12
)-(
1.13
)-(
1.14
), we further suppose that the total density is Hölder continuous on both sides of
\Sigma_{0}=\partial D_{0}
, across which it may be discontinuous:

(1.18)

\rho_{0}\in\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\;\text{ where }\;\alpha\in(0,1).

We denote by
\Pi
the Cauchy’s tensor

\Pi=2\mu(\rho,c)\mathbb{D}u+\big(\lambda(\rho,c)\dvg u-P(\rho,c)+\widetilde{P}\big)I_{3}.

When
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
, we additionally assume that there exists
\check{\alpha}\in(0,3/2-\alpha)
such that (
\dot{v}=\partial_{t}v+(u\cdot\nabla)v
)

(1.19)

\displaystyle(\rho\dot{u})_{|t=0}=\dvg(\Pi)_{|t=0}\in\dot{H}^{-\check{\alpha}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

If
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, we will instead take
\check{\alpha}=1
.

Our result reads as follows:

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume the above assumptions hold for the constitutive coefficients and the initial data. There exist constants

\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that if

(1.20)

\displaystyle\lVert P(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{P}\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\lVert\rho_{0}u_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}\cap L^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\lVert\nabla u_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}\leqslant\epsilon,

and

\diamond

either

\lVert\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})-\widetilde{\mu}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime},

\diamond

or (
1.6
) holds and

(1.21)

\displaystyle\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0}),\;P(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\partial D_{0})}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime},

then there exists a unique global-in-time weak solution
(c,\rho,u)
to the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.11
) verifying:

\displaystyle P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P},\;u,\;\nabla u\in L^{\infty}((0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}));

\displaystyle\dot{u},\;\sigma^{\frac{\check{\alpha}}{2}}\nabla\dot{u}\in L^{2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}),\,\text{ and }\,\sigma^{\frac{\check{\alpha}}{2}}\dot{u},\;\sigma^{\frac{1+\check{\alpha}}{2}}\nabla\dot{u}\in L^{\infty}((0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}));

\displaystyle\sigma^{\frac{1+\check{\alpha}}{2}}\ddot{u}\in L^{2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3});\;\text{ where }\;\sigma(t)=\min\{1,t\}.

Additionally, we have

\nabla u\in L^{1}((0,\infty),L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

and hence the velocity admits a well-defined flow map. This flow map transports the initial domain
D_{0}
to a
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regular domain
D=D(t)
and

\int_{0}^{\infty}\lVert\nabla u(t^{\prime})\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D(t^{\prime})}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}dt^{\prime}<\infty,\;\;\text{ and, \;for all }\;\;t\in(0,\infty),\;\,\rho(t)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

Furthermore, if (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) hold, then the jumps of the pressure, and the velocity gradient across
\partial D(t)
decay exponentially to zero in time.

The proof of Theorem
1.2
is presented in Section
4
, based on the a priori
estimates in Section
2
whose proofs is given in Section
3
.

Remark 1.3
.

The solution possesses additional regularity and decay rates described in Section
2
.

\diamond

In particular, the classical energy controls the volume of the moving domain
D(t)
as follows:

(1.22)

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in(0,\infty),\;\;\left\lvert D(t)\right\rvert\leqslant\left\lvert D_{0}\right\rvert+(\widetilde{P})^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{0}(x)\left(\dfrac{\left\lvert u_{0}(x)\right\rvert^{2}}{2}+\int_{\widetilde{\rho}_{+}\mathbb{1}_{D_{0}}(x)}^{\rho_{0}(x)}s^{-2}\left(P(s,c_{0}(x))-c_{0}(x)\widetilde{P}\right)ds\right)dx.

This estimate is by no means predictable. Above,
\widetilde{\rho}_{+}
denotes the constant equilibrium state of the density of the fluid in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus D(t)
; it satisfies

P(\widetilde{\rho}_{+},1)=\widetilde{P}.

Likewise, define
\widetilde{\rho}_{-}
by

P(\widetilde{\rho}_{-},0)=\widetilde{P}.

For brevity, we will write
\widetilde{\rho}(c)
in place of
\widetilde{\rho}_{\pm}
such that

P(\widetilde{\rho}(c),c)=\widetilde{P}.

The smallness conditions on the pressure fluctuation (
1.20
)–(
1.21
) mean that the
"+"-fluid density is close to
\widetilde{\rho}_{+}
, while the "-"-fluid density is close to
\widetilde{\rho}_{-}
.

\diamond

The low-frequency assumption (see (
1.14
)) helps to derive sufficient time-decay estimates for the solution, which in turn imply the following uniform-in-time
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
-regularity of
D
:

\sup_{t>0}\Big(\left\lVert\partial D(t)\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}+\left\lvert\partial D(t)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}+\left\lvert\partial D(t)\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}^{-1}\Big)<\infty.

\diamond

Finally, note that assumption (
1.19
) implies

\nabla u_{0}\in L^{6/(1+2\check{\alpha})}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

In general, this is the maximal integrability implied by (
1.19
).

Remark 1.4
.

A cornerstone of the proof of Theorem
1.2
is to derive a Lipschitz bound for the velocity. This is the minimal regularity needed to propagate the sharp interface’s regularity. To achieve this, we express the velocity
gradient as (see (
2.6
)):

\displaystyle\nabla u

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\bigg)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\Bigg(\frac{P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)

(1.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)\bigg),

where
\mathcal{K}
and
\check{\mathcal{K}}
are combinations of second-order Riesz operators.

\diamond

As a first step, we perform energy estimates (
à
la Hoff) for
\dot{u}
, which yield

L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
estimate for
\dot{u}
, with
p\in(3,\infty)
. These energy estimates involve
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
norm of the velocity gradient and the pressure fluctuation (with
q\in\{3,4,6\}
). To derive these bounds, we use the fact that Riesz operators are continuous on
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In particular, the norms of the first two terms of (
1.23
) are obtained via an interpolation inequality, whereas the third is bounded by

\left\lVert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\Bigg(\frac{P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant\frac{C}{\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu(\rho,c)}\left\lVert P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Next, we observe that

\dfrac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}H_{q-1}(\rho,c)dx+\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}\right\rvert^{q}dx\leqslant\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left\lvert F\right\rvert^{q}dx,

where

\rho\partial_{\rho}H_{q-1}(\rho,c)-H_{q-1}(\rho,c)=\left\lvert P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}\right\rvert^{q-2}(P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}),

and

F=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})+[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}](2\mathbb{D}u).

As for the first two terms of (
1.23
), we use an interpolation inequality to obtain an estimate for the
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of the first term of
F
. Next, we prove that the
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-norm of the last term of
F
, as well as the last terms in (
1.23
) are small compared to the
L^{q}
-norm of
\nabla u
,
provided the jump of the shear viscosity
\mu(\rho,c)
is small in
L^{\infty}(\partial D)
. In total,
one can close the energy estimate for
\dot{u}
, assuming the shear viscosity is piecewise
Hölder regular, its jump across
\partial D
is small; and the density is upper bounded and far away from vacuum. Note that we use time weights, which makes the computations more technical than the heuristic above.

Let us notice that for constant shear viscosity coefficient, the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound for
\dot{u}

immediately yields the propagation of the
L^{\infty}
-bound of the density (see
[
23
]
). However, this seems not to hold in the case of density-dependent shear viscosity (see
[
43
]
), or in the case of general viscous tensor (see
[
4
,
3
]
). Indeed, the pressure and the velocity gradient appear to be of the same order, and propagating the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound of the density requires a Lipschitz velocity.

\diamond

Given the integrability of
\dot{u}
obtained above, the first term in (
1.23
)
belongs to
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In fact, modulo the multiplicative factor
1/\mu(\rho,c)
, this term is Hölder continuous on the whole space. The other terms are even-order Riesz transform of discontinuous functions, and it is well-known that even-order Riesz operators are not continuous on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Recent results (see
[
16
,
17
]
) prove that these operators are actually continuous on
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, with
p\in(1,\infty)
, with a norm depending on
\left\lVert\partial D(t)\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}
,
\left\lvert\partial D(t)\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}
and
\left\lvert\partial D(t)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}
. These quantities are known to grow exponentially with the time integral of the Lipschitz norm of the velocity: for instance

\left\lVert\partial D(t)\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}\leqslant\left\lVert\partial D_{0}\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}},\quad\left\lvert\partial D(t)\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}\geqslant\left\lvert\partial D_{0}\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}.

Thus, a non-uniform-in-time estimate for

(1.24)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

leads to an exponential growth in time. For the one-phase model (Navier–Stokes system for compressible fluid), this exponential growth is compensated by the exponential-in-time decay of the pressure and velocity jumps.
For the two-phase model, we are able to prove the exponential-in-time decay for the pressure and velocity gradient jumps under the assumptions (
1.9
)-(
1.10
). This decay does not hold in general, as we will discuss in the following remark. Our strategy is to use the low-frequency assumption (
1.14
) to derive uniform-in-time estimate for (
1.24
).

Leveraging the continuity of Riesz operators on
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we prove that
the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound (resp.
\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound) of the last two terms of (
1.23
) is small compared to the
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound (resp.
\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound) of
\nabla u
; provided that the viscosity jump is small in
L^{\infty}(\partial D)
. We apply similar arguments to derive piecewise Hölder estimate for
F
, from which the propagation of the density’s
piecewise Hölder regularity follows, since we have (from the mass equation):

\partial_{t}f(\rho,c)+u\cdot\nabla f(\rho,c)+P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}=-F,\;\text{ where }\;\rho\partial_{\rho}f(\rho,c)=\nu(\rho,c).

Above, the pressure fluctuation term is treated as a damping term.

Remark 1.5
.

We consider a simplified case of (
1.1
) in which the two phases have the same constant viscosity coefficient but different power-law pressures:

\mu(\rho,c)=\mu,\;\,\lambda(\rho,c)=\lambda,\;\text{ and }\;P(\rho,c)=A(c)\rho^{\beta(c)}.

From the mass equation, one readily obtains

(2\mu+\lambda)\big(\partial_{t}\log P(\rho,c)+u\cdot\nabla\log P(\rho,c)\big)+\beta(c)\big(P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}\big)=\beta(c)(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u}),

and the jump across the moving interface is:

\displaystyle(2\mu+\lambda)\dfrac{d}{dt}\llbracket\log\big(P(\rho,c)\big)\circ\tau_{j}\rrbracket

\displaystyle+\left\langle\beta(c_{0}\circ\tau_{0,j})\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}\llbracket\big(P(\rho,c)\big)\circ\tau_{j}\rrbracket

(1.25)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\beta(c_{0}\circ\tau_{0,j})\rrbracket\Big((-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\circ\tau_{j}-\left\langle P(\rho,c)\circ\tau_{j}\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}+\widetilde{P}\Big).

Similarly, from the mass equation we obtain

(2\mu+\lambda)\big[\partial_{t}\log\rho+u\cdot\nabla\log\rho\big]+P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u}),

and hence

(1.26)

\displaystyle(2\mu+\lambda)\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle\log(\rho\circ\tau_{j})\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\circ\tau_{j}-\left\langle P(\rho,c)\circ\tau_{j}\right\rangle_{\text{avg}}+\widetilde{P}.

From (
1.25
)-(
1.26
), it follows that

\dfrac{d}{dt}\bigg\llbracket\log\big(A(c)^{\frac{2}{\beta(1)+\beta(0))}}\rho\big)\circ\tau_{j}\bigg\rrbracket+\frac{1}{2\mu+\lambda}\llbracket\big(P(\rho,c)\big)\circ\tau_{j}\rrbracket=0.

Under the assumption (
1.6
), the jumps appearing above are comparable in the sense that

\inf\Bigg(\frac{\llbracket\big(P(\rho,c)\big)\circ\tau_{j}\rrbracket}{\llbracket\log\big(A(c)^{\frac{2}{\beta(1)+\beta(0))}}\rho\big)\circ\tau_{j}\rrbracket}\Bigg)>0

and the exponential-in-time decay of jumps follows. Otherwise, it is unclear whether the exponential-in-time holds in general. Specifically, under the assumption
A(c)=1
, we should require

\frac{\varrho_{1}^{\beta(1)}-\varrho_{2}^{\beta(0)}}{\log(\varrho_{1})-\log(\varrho_{2})}>0,\quad\text{for}\;\varrho_{1}\;\text{close to }\;\widetilde{\rho}(1),\;\text{ and }\;\varrho_{2}\;\text{ close to }\;\widetilde{\rho}(0);

which does not hold in general.

1.2.
Review of known results

Understanding the interaction between two or more fluids has become a central issue in modern science. Mixture flow occurs in various contexts: biological systems (blood, respiratory tract fluid); geophysical phenomena (ice formation, river flooding, landslides, and snow-slides) and industrial applications (flows in chemical reactors or nuclear reactor channels), to name a few examples. For more interesting examples, we refer to
[
26
]
. The constituents of the mixture
(or phases) may have different densities, pressure laws, temperatures and viscosities.

An interesting problem is the study of the dynamic of the interface separating the components of a mixture. From a physical point of view, the interface is a narrow layer across which the fluid states (density, temperature) may experience large variations or discontinuities, giving rise to two categories of interface models. On one hand, there are
diffuse interface models
for which the components of the mixture are separated by a thin layer (non-zero thickness). Within this thin layer, flow states experience large gradients while continuously passing from one pure zone to another. On the other hand, there are
sharp interface models
for which the interface is a hypersurface, with zero thickness. Although these models are derived from physical principles naturally, it is very expensive to study them numerically. Moreover, changes in the topology of the interface are harder to track.

The mathematical study of the sharp interface model was first addressed by Tani in 1984
[
39
]
, where the solutions were constructed in piecewise Hölder spaces. The initial density and velocity are
\mathscr{C}^{1+\alpha}
and
\mathscr{C}^{2+\alpha}
, respectively, on both sides of a suitable
\mathscr{C}^{2+\alpha}
interface. Taking into account surface tension, Denisova constructed solutions with Sobolev regularity for both initial data and interface in
[
12
]
.

In contrast to the aforementioned works based on a change in Lagrangian coordinates, other studies rely on a transformation to a flattened interface. Piecewise Sobolev-regular solutions of high order (
H^{4k},\;k\geqslant 3
) were constructed in
[
27
]
, where the interface is represented by a graph, considering scenarios with and without surface tension. The Rayleigh-Taylor instability problem has been investigated in
[
28
,
29
]
, with the latter reference addressing the zero surface tension limit problem.
Finally, akin to the previous works, there is a study by Kubo, Shibata and Soga
[
32
]
that relies on the maximal regularity property of the linearized system.

Note that only Tani’s work addresses density-dependent viscosity coefficients, and the result is only local in time.
The other results concern cases where each phase of the flow has constant viscosity coefficients, possibly different.
Existence of global-in-time solutions is established in this latter case under smallness of the piecewise
H^{4k},\;k\geqslant 3
norm, and with almost flat interface. The present paper allows for density-dependent viscosity coefficients and low regular initial data and interface and establishes global-in-time result.

In the aforementioned papers, the system is solved on either side of the sharp interface, with a transmission boundary condition. A different approach to tracking the interface is to construct weak solutions for the full model in a class that allows for the study of its dynamic. The construction of weak solutions in the classical energy framework to (
1.1
) was first addressed in 2019 by Vasseur, Wen, and Yu (see
[
40
]
), in the case where
the viscosities coefficients are constant and common for both phases
. The authors considered power-law for the pressure of the phases, with a closeness constraint on the adiabatic exponents. This result has been improved by Novotný
[
38
]
, Wen
[
42
]
and Kra
\check{c}
mar, Kwon, Ne
\check{c}
esová, Novotný
[
31
]
by allowing a
more general pressure law, and also by Novotný and Pokorný
[
37
]
for multi-component fluid models. It is important to note that although the pressure laws
of the components of the mixture are different, the viscosities of the components are supposed to be the same constant.
The solutions that they constructed are too weak to track down the discontinuity in the density or to study the dynamic of the
interface between the components of the mixture.

In one-dimensional space, Bresch, Burtea and Lagoutière
[
6
,
5
]
achieved global well-posedness for non-common constant viscosity, with numerical considerations in
[
7
]
. However, for higher dimensions,
d\in\{2,3\}
, it has been explained for instance in
[
3
,
4
,
9
]
that, even the algebraic structure of the anisotropic one-phase model completely eludes the theories developed in the isotropic case with constant viscosity coefficients
[
14
,
23
,
35
]
.
The issue stems from the complete degeneration of the quantity (namely, the effective flux) that links the velocity’s divergence to the pressure, as it is now of the same order as the velocity gradient.

In a recent paper (see
[
44
]
), we establish the local-in-time well-posedness for the system
(
1.1
) in a functional framework tailored to our analysis. This serves as a building block
for proving the existence and uniqueness of discontinuous solutions to the two-dimensional (one-phase) Navier–Stokes system
for a compressible fluid with density-dependent viscosity (see
[
43
]
). We account for
viscosity coefficients arising from homogenization (see, e.g.,
[
20
,
19
]
),
which are not covered by the work of Bresch and Desjardins
[
8
]
. Notice that the proof relies strongly on
the fact that the jumps of the pressure and the velocity gradient across the discontinuity curve decay exponentially in time.
As discussed in Remark
1.5
above, this exponential-in-time decay does not seem to hold in general for the two-fluid model.
Thus, we impose a low-frequency condition on the initial data to derive sufficient decay for the solution, so that exponential decay of the jumps is not needed. Moreover, unlike the aforementioned result (see
[
43
]
), which imposes
smallness of the shear viscosity fluctuation in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\partial D_{0}}
, Theorem
1.2
only requires
the smallness of the shear viscosity fluctuation in
L^{\infty}
and, in some cases, only of its jump.

We conclude this section by noting that, for the two dimensional incompressible model, a smallness condition on the viscosity fluctuation also appears in
[
17
,
18
]
. Recent work (see
[
34
]
) shows that, when the interface has low regularity (
\partial D_{0}\in W^{2,2+\varepsilon}
, for some
\varepsilon
in a sharp range
(0,\varepsilon_{0})
), the smallness on the viscosity fluctuation can be replaced by a smallness of the initial data. In this work, the key argument depends heavily on the fact that the system is considered in the two dimensional space. The extension to three dimensional space, or to the propagation of
W^{2,p}
-regularity (with
p\in(2+\varepsilon_{0},\infty)
) of interfaces, or to the compressible model
appears particularly involved.

Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we state all the a priori estimates
needed for the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Their proofs are presented in Section
3
.
Finally, we prove Theorem
1.2
in Section
4
.

2.
Statement of a priori estimates

This section is devoted to deriving all the key a priori estimates needed for the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
These estimates consist of piecewise Hölder and energy-type bounds for approximate solutions of system (
2.1
) below,
from which the global-in-time persistence of the regularity of the discontinuity surface will follow. This final step, concluding the proof of Theorem
1.2
, is the focus of Section
4
below.

More precisely, we consider the following Cauchy problem (we temporarily omit the superscript
n
):

(2.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}c+u\cdot\nabla c=0,\\
\partial_{t}\rho+\dvg(\rho u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\dvg(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla P(\rho,c)=\dvg\big(2\mu(\rho,c)\mathbb{D}u\big)+\nabla\big(\lambda(\rho,c)\dvg u\big),\\
(c,\rho,u)_{|t=0}=(c_{0},\rho_{0},u_{0}^{n}),\end{cases}

where
\big(u_{0}^{n}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of initial velocities satisfying the same assumptions as
u_{0}
in Theorem
1.2
, along with the compatibility condition

(2.2)

(\rho\dot{u})_{|t=0}=\dvg\big(2\mu(\rho_{0},c_{0})\mathbb{D}u_{0}^{n}+\big(\lambda(\rho_{0},c_{0})\dvg u_{0}^{n}-P(\rho_{0},c_{0})+\widetilde{P}\big)I_{3}\big)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).

The construction of the sequence
\big(u_{0}^{n}\big)_{n}
and the proof of its convergence toward

u_{0}
are given in Section
4
below. Our main objective is to derive estimate for

\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{1}((0,t),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))}
, from which we will deduce the global-in-time
existence of the solution, as well as the persistence of interface regularity. This will be the focus of the
next two subsections.

2.1.
Piecewise Hölder bounds

This subsection is devoted to deriving piecewise Hölder estimates for the velocity gradient and the pressure fluctuation.
The first step in obtaining these bounds is to derive an expression for the velocity gradient. To this end, we begin by
rewriting the momentum equations
\eqref{ep2.1}_{3}
as follows
(
hereafter,
G(\rho,c)
denotes the pressure fluctuation, namely
G(\rho,c)=P(\rho,c)-\widetilde{P}
)
:

(2.3)

\widetilde{\mu}\Delta u+\nabla\big((\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda(\rho,c))\dvg u-G(\rho,c)\big)=\rho\dot{u}-\dvg\big(2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\big).

Next, we apply the Leray projector,
\mathbb{P}
, to obtain:

\widetilde{\mu}\Delta\mathbb{P}u=\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})-\dvg\mathbb{P}\big(2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\big),

and whence

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}\nabla\mathbb{P}u

\displaystyle=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\mathbb{P}\big(2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\big)

\displaystyle=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})-(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\nabla\mathbb{P}u+\big[\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\mathbb{P},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u).

It follows that

(2.4)

\mu(\rho,c)\nabla\mathbb{P}u=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})+\big[\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\mathbb{P},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u).

Similarly, applying the divergence operator to (
2.3
) yields

\Delta\big((2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda(\rho,c))\dvg u-G(\rho,c)\big)=\dvg(\rho\dot{u})-\dvg\dvg\big(2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\big),

and hence

\displaystyle(2\widetilde{\mu}+\lambda(\rho,c))\dvg u-G(\rho,c)

\displaystyle=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})+(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg\big(2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\mathbb{D}u\big)

\displaystyle=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})-2(\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu})\dvg u

\displaystyle+\big[(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg,\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u).

It follows that

(2.5)

\big(2\mu(\rho,c)+\lambda(\rho,c)\big)\dvg u-G(\rho,c)=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})+\big[(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg,\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u).

Combining (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), we obtain the following expression for the velocity gradient:

\displaystyle\nabla u

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathbb{P}u+\nabla(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{P})u

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathbb{P}u-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\dvg u

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\bigg)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\Bigg(\frac{G(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)

(2.6)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)\bigg).

Above,
\mathcal{K}
and
\check{\mathcal{K}}
denote the following even order singular operators:

\mathcal{K}=\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\mathbb{P}\;\text{ and }\,\check{\mathcal{K}}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg\dvg.

We now assume that the density and the shear viscosity coefficients are bounded from above and below as follows:

(2.7)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle 0<\rho_{*}:=\inf_{t,x}\rho(t,x)\leqslant\sup_{t,x}\rho(t,x):=\rho^{*}<\infty;\\
\displaystyle 0<\mu_{*}:=\inf_{t,x}\mu(\rho,c))(t,x)\leqslant\sup_{t,x}\mu(\rho,c))(t,x)=:\mu^{*}<\infty.\end{cases}

Also, given that the pressure is strictly increasing with respect to the density, we have the following bounds

(2.8)

\displaystyle 0<a_{*}(c):=\inf_{\rho\in[\rho_{*},\rho^{*}]}\Bigg(\frac{\rho\partial_{\rho}P(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)\leqslant\sup_{\rho\in[\rho_{*},\rho^{*}]}\Bigg(\frac{\rho\partial_{\rho}P(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)=:a^{*}(c)<\infty,

from which we define the ratio:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\overline{a}=\sup_{c}\frac{a^{*}(c)}{a_{*}(c)}.

Throughout what follows,
C
will denote a generic constant that is independent of the bounds of the solutions.
It may depend on
\widetilde{P}
,
\widetilde{\mu}
,
\widetilde{\lambda}
,
\alpha
, or arise from embedding inequalities etc.
In contrast,
C_{*}
will depend on
\rho^{*}
,
\mu_{*}
,
\mu^{*}
. Both
C
and
C_{*}
may vary from line to line.

Let us denote by
\varphi(t)=\varphi_{0}(\mathcal{X}^{-1}(t))
the level-set function defining
D(t)
:

D(t)=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\colon\varphi(t,x)>0\big\},\;\text{ and let }\Sigma(t)=\partial D(t).

Under a smallness condition on the shear viscosity jump we establish, in the following lemma, bounds for the functionals
\vartheta
, and
\check{\vartheta}
defined as (we recall the definition of the
(\rho,c)
-dependent function
f
in (
1.7
), which we supplement with
f(\widetilde{\rho}_{+},1)=f(\widetilde{\rho}_{-},0)=0
):

\displaystyle\check{\vartheta}(t)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\Sigma(t)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}+\left\lvert\nabla\varphi(t)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}}+\left\lvert f(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u,G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})};

\displaystyle\vartheta(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert G(\rho,c),\,\nabla u,\,\nabla\mathbb{P}u,\,\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](\mathbb{D}u),\,\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](\mathbb{D}u)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Our result reads as follows.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let us introduce the functionals
\phi_{\Sigma}
,
\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}
and
\delta
defined by

(2.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\phi_{\Sigma}(t)\displaystyle&=\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c),\,\lambda(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell_{\Sigma}^{-\alpha/2}\left\lVert\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu},\,\lambda(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\lambda}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Big),\\
\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}(t)&=\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}^{\alpha/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha/2}}\big)\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big),\\
\delta(t)&=\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c),\,\nu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{p}(\Sigma)}\end{cases}

where
\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}
is a polynomial in
\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert
,
\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}
and
\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}^{-1}
.

We now fix some
p\in(6/\alpha,\infty)
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that if

\displaystyle\frac{4C}{\mu_{*}}\Bigg[\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big(1+\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\llbracket\nu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)

(2.11)

\displaystyle+\sup_{[0,t]}\Big(\overline{a}\left\lvert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}(\Sigma)}+\frac{\mu^{*}}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\llbracket\nu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\Bigg]\leqslant 1,

then the following inequalities hold true:

\diamond

Piecewise Hölder estimate
:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\check{\vartheta}(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\Bigg(\check{\vartheta}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg)\exp\Bigg[C_{*}K_{0}e^{C\vartheta(t)}\Bigg(\vartheta(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\Bigg)\Bigg],

where
K_{0}
depends on
\left\lVert\Sigma_{0}\right\rVert_{\text{Lip}}
,
\left\lvert\nabla\varphi_{0}\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}^{-1}
,
\left\lvert\Sigma_{0}\right\rvert_{\text{inf}}
, and
\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert
.

\diamond

Lipschitz estimate:

\displaystyle\vartheta(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}\Bigg\{\left\lVert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\Sigma}\left\lVert G(\rho,c),\,\nabla u,\,(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\Big(1+\delta(t)e^{\alpha\vartheta(t)}\Big)\Bigg[\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6/(2-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\Big(\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\delta(t)\ell_{\Sigma}^{-\alpha}\Big)\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{6/\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\check{\vartheta}(t)\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/2}(\Sigma)}\Big(1+\delta(t)e^{\alpha\vartheta(t)}\Big)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\ell_{\Sigma}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\left\lVert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{p}(\Sigma)}+\left\lvert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}(\Sigma)}\Big)

(2.13)

\displaystyle+\vartheta(t)\delta(t)\Bigg(\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}e^{\alpha\vartheta(t)}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha/2}}\big)\Bigg)\Bigg\}e^{C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert w\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}},

with

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert f(\rho,c)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\overline{a}\Bigg(\left\lVert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\delta(t)\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+C\overline{a}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6/(2-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\phi_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\big).

The proof of the above Lemma is the purpose of Section
3.1
below. The estimate of the functional
\vartheta

involves some norms of the jump of the pressure fluctuation
G(\rho,c)
. These norms can be roughly be estimated as follows:

(2.14)

\left\lVert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma)}\leqslant\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert^{1/q}\left\lVert G(\rho,c)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},\;\text{ with }q\in[1,\infty],\;\text{ and }\left\lvert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}(\Sigma)}\leqslant 2\left\lvert G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

and we will instead estimate the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bound as in (
3.18
)-(
3.24
) and we will require the pressure fluctuations to be small. In particular situations such as (
1.6
) or (
1.9
), we can derive sharper estimates for the norms of the pressure jump.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
q\in[1,\infty]
, and
0<a_{*}=\min_{c}a_{*}(c)
,
a^{*}=\max_{c}a^{*}(c)
. The following
estimates hold.

\diamond

Assume that (
1.6
) holds true. Then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma)}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{a^{*}}{a_{*}}e^{\frac{1}{q}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg(\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{0})}+C\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma)}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg),

and

\displaystyle\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lvert\llbracket f(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\llbracket G(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{a^{*}}{a_{*}}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg[\left\lvert\llbracket f(\varrho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma_{0})}+\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lvert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma)}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\ell_{\Sigma}^{-\alpha}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\big)

(2.16)

\displaystyle+C_{*}\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg].

\diamond

Assume that (
1.9
) holds true. Then we have,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma(t))}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-a_{*}t+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{0})},

and

\displaystyle\left\lvert\llbracket f(\rho,c)(t)\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma(t))}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}e^{-a_{*}t+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}}\Bigg[\left\lvert\llbracket f(\varrho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma_{0})}

(2.18)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\big(\ell_{\Sigma}^{-\alpha}+\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma)}\big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\left\lvert f(\rho,c),\,\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Big)\Bigg].

\diamond

In addition to (
1.9
), assume that (
1.10
) holds true. Then

(2.19)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\llbracket\nabla u(t)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma(t))}\leqslant C_{*}e^{-a_{*}t+C_{*}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\left\lVert\llbracket f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Sigma_{0})}\big(1+\left\lVert\nabla u(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\big).

The proof of the above lemma is the purpose of Section
3.2
below. From these bounds, we immediately deduce that the jump of
f(\rho,c)
, viscosities, pressure, and velocity gradient decay exponentially in time, once the functionals

\vartheta
and
\check{\vartheta}
are uniformly bounded in time. The estimate obtained for these functionals in Lemma
2.1
above, involves the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
norm of
\nabla u
,
G(\rho,c)
, with
p\in(6/\alpha,\infty)
; and the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
norm of the material acceleration
\dot{u}
. In the following section, we derive these bounds from time-weighted energy estimates.

2.2.
Time-weighted energy estimates

This section is devoted to deriving
L^{1}((0,t),L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
-bounds for the velocity gradient, pressure fluctuation, and for the material acceleration as these appear in the estimates of the functionals
\vartheta
and
\check{\vartheta}
in Lemma
2.1
. These
L^{1}((0,t),L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
-bounds will follow from the fact that the following energy functionals are uniformly bounded in time:

(2.20)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)&=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{1+r_{0}}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,t]}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{1+r_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(2\mu(\rho,c)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\rho,c)\left\lvert\dvg u\right\rvert^{2}\big),\\
\mathcal{A}_{2}(t)&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,t]}\sigma\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\frac{3}{2}(1+r_{0})}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\frac{3}{2}(1+r_{0})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(2\mu(\rho,c)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}\dot{u}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\rho,c)\left\lvert\dvg\dot{u}\right\rvert^{2}\big),\\
\mathcal{A}_{3}(t)&=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sigma^{1+\check{\alpha}}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\frac{3}{2}(1+r_{0})}\left\lVert\sqrt{\rho}\ddot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{[0,t]}\sigma^{1+\check{\alpha}}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\frac{3}{2}(1+r_{0})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big(2\mu(\rho,c)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}\dot{u}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\rho,c)\left\lvert\dvg\dot{u}\right\rvert^{2}\big).\end{cases}

Above
0<r_{0}<\frac{3}{p_{0}}-\frac{5}{2}
(we recall that
1<p_{0}<6/5
, see (
1.14
));
\check{\alpha}\in(0,1]
is introduced in (
1.19
); and the time weights
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
and
\sigma
are defined by:

\sigma(t)=\min\{1,t\},\;\text{ and }\;\left\langle t\right\rangle=\sqrt{1+t^{2}}.

From now on, the constants
C
and
C_{*}
may also depend on
r_{0}
, and
\check{\alpha}
.
C_{0}
will stand for a constant depending on
\left\lVert\rho_{0}u_{0},\,G(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rVert_{L^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}
and on the initial energies
E_{0}
and
\check{E}_{0}
(see their definitions in ) as positive powers. We denote by
\check{C}_{0}
a constant that depends on the same quantities as
C_{0}
, and additionally on
\left\lVert\nabla u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}
and
\lVert(\rho\dot{u})_{|t=0}\rVert_{\dot{H}^{-\check{\alpha}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}
.

We obtain the following bounds.

Lemma 2.3
.

There exist constants
C=C(\alpha)>0
and
c_{*}=c_{*}(\rho_{*},\rho^{*})
such that if

(2.21)

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\leqslant 1,\quad\text{and}\quad E_{0}\leqslant c_{*},

then the following estimates holds true:

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)\leqslant C_{*}\Big(C_{0}\big(1+\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{6}{\alpha}}E_{0}\big)+\left\lVert\nabla u_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+E^{\frac{1}{4}}_{0}\big(\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)^{\frac{5}{4}}+C_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}}\big)\Big),

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}(t)\leqslant C_{*}C_{0}\Big(1+\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)^{2}\Big)+C_{*}\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)\Big(1+\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)^{2}+\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{6}{\alpha}}C_{0}\Big),

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}(t)\leqslant C_{*}\Big(\check{C}_{0}+\mathcal{L}\bigg(C_{0}\phi_{\Sigma}^{\frac{2}{\alpha}},\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)\bigg)\Big)e^{C_{*}C_{0}\big(1+\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{6}{\alpha}}+\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)\big)},

where
\mathcal{L}
is a polynomial.

The proof of the above lemma is the purpose of Section
3.4
. Prior to that, we derive in Section
3.3
some basic a priori estimates for the solutions to the system (
2.1
). From these estimates, we immediately deduce:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
p\in[6,\infty)
. There exists a constant
\check{k}=\check{k}(p,\rho_{*},\rho^{*})>0
, such that if

C\frac{\check{k}}{\mu_{*}}\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\leqslant\frac{1}{2},

then we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lVert\nabla u,\,G(\rho,c)\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\left\lVert\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6/(2-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Big)\leqslant C_{*}C_{0}\Bigg(1+\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{3}{\alpha}\big(1-\frac{2}{p}\big)}\Bigg)+C_{*}\big(\mathcal{A}_{1}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big);

and

\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant C_{*}\check{\mathcal{L}}\big(\check{C}_{0},C_{0}\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{2}{\alpha}},\check{\phi}_{\Sigma}^{\frac{6}{\alpha}}\mathcal{A}_{1}(t),\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(t),\,\mathcal{A}_{3}(t)\big),

where
\check{\mathcal{L}}
is a polynomial.

The proof of Lemma
2.4
is the purpose of Section
3.5
below. This concludes the present section.

All the a priori estimates derived in this section will be closed with the help of a bootstrap argument in Section
4
. We also prove the compactness of the approximate solutions, thereby obtaining Theorem
1.2
.

3.
Proofs of the a priori estimates

3.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.1

We begin the proof of Lemma
2.1
by rewriting the expression of the velocity gradient (see (
2.6
)):

\displaystyle\nabla u=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\bigg)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\Bigg(\frac{G(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)

(3.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)\bigg).

Proof of (
2.12
).

We estimate each term appearing on the RHS of the expression above as follows:

\diamond

First term
: The first term in (
3.1
) is estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mu_{*}^{2}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lVert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}^{2}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lVert\nabla\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\left\lVert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\mathbb{P}(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}^{2}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

We used the Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality
\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant\lVert\nabla g\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}
, the embedding

\dot{W}^{1,3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the continuity of Riesz transforms on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, with
p\in(1,\infty)
.

\diamond

Second and third terms
: The second term in (
3.1
) is a second-order Riesz transform of a discontinuous function. Thus, we apply (
A.45
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\bigg)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\left\lvert\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+C\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert\nu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Bigg).

We derive a similar estimate for the third term

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\Bigg(\frac{G(\rho,c)}{\nu(\rho,c)}\Bigg)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert G(\rho,c)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert\nu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Bigg).

\diamond

Remaining terms
: Using (
3
), the next-to-last term in (
3.1
) is estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mu_{*}^{2}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lVert\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mu_{*}^{2}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lVert\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\big)\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{P}u,\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.2)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

The same estimate also holds for
\lvert\frac{1}{\mu(\rho,c)}[\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla,\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}](2\mathbb{D}u)\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
.

Similarly, we apply (
A.45
)-(
3
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\bigg(\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u)\bigg)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\frac{1}{\nu(\rho,c)}\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert\nu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Big)\left\lVert\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\big)\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Combining all these estimates, we arrive at:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}\psi(s)\Big(\frac{1}{\mu_{*}}\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)(s),\nu(\rho,c)(s)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(s)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma(s)}\big|\Sigma(s)\big|_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\big)\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{P}u,\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\mathbb{D}u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C}{\mu_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

where

\psi=\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{1/2}+\left\lVert G(\rho,c),\big[\mathcal{K},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u,\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big]\mathbb{D}u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

To estimate the last term above, we first observe that the
(\rho,c)
-dependent function
f=f(\rho,c)
satisfies

\partial_{t}f(\rho,c)+u\cdot\nabla f(\rho,c)+G(\rho,c)=-F.

We write the above equation along the velocity flow map
\mathcal{X}
of the velocity and we obtain

(3.4)

\partial_{t}f\big(\varrho(t,y),c_{0}(y)\big)+G\big(\varrho(t,y),c_{0}(y)\big)=-F\big(t,\mathcal{X}(t,y)\big),

where
\varrho(t,y)=\rho(t,\mathcal{X}(t,y)
. Let
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
be two points located on the same side of
\Sigma
. Evaluating (
3.4
) at
y\in\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
and taking the difference, we obtain:

\partial_{t}f\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}+g(t,y_{1},y_{2})f\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}=-F\big(t,\mathcal{X}(t,y_{j})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2},

where (notice that
c_{0}(y_{1})=c_{0}(y_{2})
)

g(t,y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{G\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}}{f\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle f\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}

\displaystyle=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}g(\tau,y_{1},y_{2})d\tau}f\big(\rho_{0}(y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}

(3.5)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{\tau}^{t}g(\tau^{\prime},y_{1},y_{2})d\tau^{\prime}}F\big(\tau,\mathcal{X}(\tau,y_{j})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}d\tau,

and

\displaystyle G\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}

\displaystyle=g(t,y_{1},y_{2})e^{-\int_{0}^{t}g(\tau,y_{1},y_{2})d\tau}f\big(\rho_{0}(y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}

\displaystyle-g(t,y_{1},y_{2})\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\int_{\tau}^{t}g(\tau^{\prime},y_{1},y_{2})d\tau^{\prime}}F\big(\tau,\mathcal{X}(\tau,y_{j})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}d\tau.

Under the assumption (
2.8
), we have (recall that
c_{0}(y_{1})=c_{0}(y_{2})
)

g(t,y_{1},y_{2})\in\big[a_{*}(c_{0}(y_{1})),a^{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))\big],

and hence

\displaystyle\left\lvert G\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant a^{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))e^{-ta_{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))}\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lvert y_{2}-y_{1}\right\rvert^{\alpha}

(3.6)

\displaystyle+a^{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-\tau)a_{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))}\left\lvert F(\tau)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(\tau)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(\tau,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(\tau,y_{1})\right\rvert^{\alpha}d\tau.

Recalling that the velocity flow map satisfies

\mathcal{X}(t,y_{j})=y_{j}+\int_{0}^{t}u\big(s,\mathcal{X}(s,y_{j})\big)ds,

we deduce that for all
0\leqslant t^{\prime}\leqslant t

\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t^{\prime},y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t^{\prime},y_{1})\right\rvert\leqslant\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t,y_{1})\right\rvert+\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(s,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(s,y_{1})\right\rvert ds,

and Grönwall’s Lemma yields

\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t^{\prime},y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t^{\prime},y_{1})\right\rvert\leqslant e^{\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t,y_{1})\right\rvert.

Returning to (
3.6
), we find that

\displaystyle\left\lvert G\big(\varrho(t,y_{j}),c_{0}(y_{1})\big)\Big|_{j=1}^{j=2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant a^{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))e^{-ta_{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t,y_{1})\right\rvert^{\alpha}

\displaystyle+a^{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))\left\lvert\mathcal{X}(t,y_{2})-\mathcal{X}(t,y_{1})\right\rvert^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-\tau)a_{*}(c_{0}(y_{1}))+\alpha\int_{\tau}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert F(\tau)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(\tau)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}d\tau,

and hence

\displaystyle\left\lvert G(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\overline{D}(t))}

\displaystyle\leqslant\kappa^{*}(0)e^{-a_{*}(0)t+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+a^{*}(0)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-\tau)a_{*}(0)+\alpha\int_{\tau}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert F(\tau)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(\tau)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}d\tau,

and

\displaystyle\left\lvert G(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus D(t))}

\displaystyle\leqslant a^{*}(1)e^{-a_{*}(1)t+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+a^{*}(1)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-\tau)a_{*}(1)+\alpha\int_{\tau}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u(s)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds}\left\lvert F(\tau)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(\tau)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}d\tau.

Then, we deduce (recalling (
3.5
))

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg(\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert F(\tau)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg).

To obtain an estimate for the piecewise Hölder semi-norm of

(3.8)

F=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\dvg(\rho\dot{u})+\big[\check{\mathcal{K}},\mu(\rho,c)-\widetilde{\mu}\big](2\mathbb{D}u),

we use the embedding
\dot{W}^{1,3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and we follow the computations leading to (
3.2
) and we arrive at

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg(\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+C\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\big(\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\big)\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+C\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert\mu(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.9)

\displaystyle+C\overline{a}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\sup_{[0,t]}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Now, we combine the above estimate with (
3.3
), and we use the smallness condition on the shear viscosity jump (
2.1
) to derive (recall that
\lvert\mu(\rho,c),\nu(\rho,c)\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leqslant C_{*}\lvert f(\rho,c)\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\beta}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
):

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u,G(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg(\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+C_{*}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Big)\Big(\psi+\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{P}u,\,\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Big)

\displaystyle+C_{*}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

With the help of Young’s and interpolation inequalities, we estimate the last term above as follows (
\eta>0
):

\displaystyle C_{*}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}e^{\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha/2}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C_{*}}{\eta}e^{2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha/2}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}\left\lVert\llbracket\mu(\rho,c)\rrbracket\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}^{2}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\frac{C_{*}}{\eta}e^{2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}\Big)\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

Choosing
\eta
small, we finally obtain:

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(\rho(t),c(t))\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma(t)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lvert\nabla u,P(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{*}e^{2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}}\Bigg[\left\lvert f(\rho_{0},c_{0})\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert\rho\dot{u}\right\rVert_{L^{3/(1-\alpha)}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.10)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\left\lvert f(\rho,c)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{\alpha}_{\text{pw},\Sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\ell^{-\alpha}_{\Sigma}+\mathfrak{P}_{\Sigma}\left\lvert\Sigma\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}\Big)\Big(\psi+\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{P}u,\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\Big)\Bigg].

We now proceed to estimate
\left\lvert\Sigma(t)\right\rvert_{\dot{\mathscr{C}}^{1+\alpha}}
,
\ell_{\Sigma(t)}^{-\alpha}
and
